### Surveys

- [`research/siena2308/`](research/siena2308/README.md) — the Sienese cipher file Archivio di Stato di Siena, Concistoro 2308 fasc. 2 "Lettere in cifra" (DECODE R4790–R4814, keys R4746–R4789), one entry per key after George Lasry's report of 17 Oct 2026; the shared solvers, the transcribed filed keys and the fasc. 1 key index ([index page](https://dbourdeau.github.io/cyphersolver/siena1421.html)).
- [`research/cryptoole/`](research/cryptoole/) — open records from Satoru's [Cryptoole](https://cryptoole.satoru.net/) index (DECODE, Cryptiana, Rosson) not yet in the catalogue, matched against the repository by R-number; also flags catalogue entries Cryptoole now lists as solved.
- [`research/qa/`](research/qa/key_split_audit.md) — QA audit (14 Oct 2026, after George Lasry's note and review): every entry checked for documents or passages in different keys presented as one result, with a proposed split for each and open questions for George.
- [`research/top50/`](research/top50/) — Schmeh's Top 50 cross-referenced entry by entry and re-checked against what has been solved since each post.
- [Thirty favourites](https://dbourdeau.github.io/cyphersolver/highlights.html) — ten funny, ten historically significant and ten ingenious findings from the write-ups, each linked to its page.
- [George Lasry's BnF cipher research](https://dbourdeau.github.io/cyphersolver/contributors.html#lasry) — 56 enciphered letters in the BnF (1452–1859), one write-up page each deciphered by George Lasry with ChatGPT Astra 6 and Anthropic Opus 5.5 (10 Oct 2026), each spot-checked here against Gallica; 55 filed as `targets/<slug>/` with `Status: no write-up`.
- [Why the famous ciphers resist](https://dbourdeau.github.io/cyphersolver/famous.html) — Kryptos, Voynich, Dorabella, Beale, Linear A, Phaistos, the pigeon message, sorted by the actual reason each holds out.

## Repository layout

```
README.md          this file
CLAUDE.md          working rules for the AI sessions (write-up, profile, shared-checkout conventions)
catalogue.json     the open targets, with scores and status; CATALOGUE.md is generated from it
archive_requests.json  gaps only an archive or a library can close (docs/archives.html); seeded from NOTES by tools/seed_archive_requests.py
CATALOGUE.md       the archival catalogue: how it was compiled, the open table, what was removed and why
SOLVED_CATALOGUE.md  what was solved or read, how, and where the AI did the work
SOLVED_RANKING.md  the solved targets ranked by difficulty and historical weight
profile.schema.json  the schema of every targets/<name>/profile.json
unsolved.htm       snapshot of Tomokiyo's source page, for diffing against later revisions

.claude/           session skills (/writeup, /profile) and the hooks that run the write-up audit
docs/              the website (GitHub Pages) and its build and check scripts, see below
lang/              shared language models: corpus registry, model registry, one n-gram engine (lang/README.md)
papers/            publication drafts;  unpublished/  pages held back from the site
decode_updates/    corrections and transcriptions queued for the DECODE database
tools/             shared scripts: transcription export (transcription_xml.py), the targets/ migration for old checkouts

targets/<name>/    one directory per cipher target: NOTES.md, profile.json, ciphertext, scripts, small derived data
research/          work that is not a single target:
  catalogue_harvest/  bulk DECODE / archive harvests that feed catalogue.json
  cryptoole/          catalogue candidates pulled from Satoru's Cryptoole index (pull.py)
  gallica_sweep/      Gallica volume sweeps for unlisted cipher leaves;  gallica_siblings/  their sibling letters
  oldest/             the search for pre-1497 ciphers (CANDIDATES.md)
  top50/              Schmeh's Top 50, cross-referenced
  mtc3/               MysteryTwister challenges, the harder track
```

The skills and checkers take a target's bare folder name (`/writeup gramont1529`, `python docs/_check_profile.py
gramont1529`) and look it up under `targets/`. A target script that puts `..` on `sys.path` still finds the shared
`lang` package through the `targets/lang/` stand-in; new scripts should use `../..`. Anything a second target could
reuse belongs in a shared directory: language models in [`lang/`](lang/), not a new `targets/<name>/lm.py`.

Every target directory has a `NOTES.md` except `targets/barney/` (a pointer to a published solution) and
`targets/richelieu/` (its record is `SOLUTION.md`). Large downloads (microfilm, corpora, OCR, run logs, images)
are excluded by [`.gitignore`](.gitignore) and regenerated by the scripts named in each directory's notes.

### The website

`docs/` is served by GitHub Pages. One manifest in [`docs/_build_site.py`](docs/_build_site.py) drives the navigation,
footers with previous/next links, "On this page" strips and the index cards. After editing any page:

```bash
cd docs && python _build_site.py
```

The builder is idempotent. It also regenerates the "At a glance" counts at the top of this file. The priority queue
on the index page is generated separately from [`docs/_queue.json`](docs/_queue.json) by `python _build_queue.py`,
and the catalogue page from `catalogue.json` by `_catalogue_page.py`. `archives.html` is built from `archive_requests.json` by `_archives_page.py`; a new `blocker: needs-physical-access` gap reaches it when `python tools/seed_archive_requests.py` is run.

Two checkers keep the ledgers in step. `python docs/_check_writeup.py <slug>` lists every surface a finished target
must reach (page, manifest, README row, ledgers, catalogue, DECODE queue) and prints `result: complete` when it has;
`--audit` lists finished targets with no write-up and the drift between README, manifest and pages.
`python docs/_check_profile.py <folder>` validates a target's `profile.json` against the schema.

Credits are data, not prose. Each profile's `outcome.credits` names who contributed what (ids in
[`contributors.json`](contributors.json); roles in `profile.schema.json`), and `outcome.provenance` says whether the
result is the project's, contributed or mixed. [`docs/_credits.py`](docs/_credits.py) reads them; the build prints a
credits line in each write-up's hero, generates `docs/contributors.html` ([`docs/_contributors_page.py`](docs/_contributors_page.py):
the table by person and kind, a card per contributor, the scholarship the work builds on, the queue of readings
waiting for an independent check) and the front page's top-contributors strip, and `writeups.html#by=<id>` filters the
write-ups to one contributor. `docs/contribute.html` and [CONTRIBUTING.md](CONTRIBUTING.md) are hand-written; the issue
forms are in `.github/ISSUE_TEMPLATE/`. Credits for work before 13 Oct 2026 were backfilled by
[`tools/backfill_credits.py`](tools/backfill_credits.py) from the key web, page bylines, merged pull requests and issues, and the notes.

The sender and recipient portraits under a write-up's title come from [`docs/_portraits.json`](docs/_portraits.json)
(slug &rarr; role, name, image, what the picture is, and its Wikimedia Commons file). Only public-domain portraits
are used, cropped to a 192-px square as `docs/portrait_<name>.jpg`. Leave a side out when the correspondent is
anonymous, an office, or has no trustworthy portrait.

Datestamps are kept in [`docs/_dates.json`](docs/_dates.json): for each page, the day the finding first landed in
the repository and the day the page last really changed. `_build_site.py` maintains the second by hashing the page
with everything it generates itself removed, so a rebuild that only restamps the furniture does not move the date;
a new page, or a new line in "Recent findings", dates itself on the next build. The file was seeded from the git
history by `python _seed_dates.py`, and any date can be corrected by editing it (the seeder never overwrites a date
already there). `python _stamp_markdown.py` writes the same dates into the result tables of this file and of
[`SOLVED_CATALOGUE.md`](SOLVED_CATALOGUE.md).

## Reproducing the two flagship results

### Armstrong → Madison, 30 August 1808

John Armstrong (U.S. Minister to France) ended a private letter to Madison with 49 groups of the diplomatic code he used
1804–1810, in which 972 = *the*. The code was never published. It was reconstructed from the State Department's own
pencil interlinear decodes on NARA microfilm M34 roll 13 (frames 0192–0201, a despatch of October 1806), merged with
Tomokiyo's known-plaintext values from the letter of 4 May 1806, and completed by alphabetical-slot inference. Reading:

> Russel ought to be the consul: he is an American by birth, and is much better qualified than any other candidate. In a
> word, he is above men in general. Next to him in fitness is O'Mealy, but he is, like Warden, an Irishman.

| file | purpose |
|---|---|
| `targets/armstrong/NOTES.md` | group-by-group evidence, corrections to the Founders transcription, residual doubts |
| `targets/armstrong/pairs.txt` | ~500 number → syllable pairs read from the pencil decodes, with frame/line reference and H/M grade |
| `targets/armstrong/code972_partial.json` | Tomokiyo's table from the 4 May 1806 known plaintext (base layer) |
| `targets/armstrong/decode972.py` | merges the three layers and renders any coded passage: `python decode972.py ps\|feb\|all\|table` |
| `targets/armstrong/export_key.py` → `key972.js` | exports the merged 580-group table for the website decoder |
| `targets/armstrong/pencil_score.py` | ranks microfilm frames by amount of faint pencil (finds the annotated despatches) |
| `targets/armstrong/crawl_wb.py` | follows Founders Online correspondent links via the Wayback Machine to list coded letters |

Not in the repo: the 393 roll-13 frames (2.5 GB, from [NARA catalog 188671172](https://catalog.archives.gov/id/188671172)),
roll 14, crops, and the downloaded Founders/LOC pages. The decode reproduces from tracked files alone:

```bash
cd armstrong && python decode972.py all
```

### Richelieu → M. de Rancé, July 1629

Homophonic substitution recovered by ciphertext-only analysis from a published transcription, then found to agree
word for word with the decipherment printed by Avenel in 1858 (*Lettres … du cardinal de Richelieu*, t. III,
pp. 368–369, 381–383). The letters are listed as undeciphered in current catalogues (cryptiana; DECODE R9461–R9462)
and should be marked solved. Full write-up: [targets/richelieu/SOLUTION.md](targets/richelieu/SOLUTION.md).

| file | purpose |
|---|---|
| `targets/richelieu/richelieu1629.txt` | S. Tomokiyo's transcription (source) |
| `targets/richelieu/parse.py` | tokenise transcription, frequency / n-gram stats |
| `targets/richelieu/build_ngrams.py` | build French quadgram model from Gutenberg texts |
| `targets/richelieu/solve.py` | simulated-annealing homophonic solver (`--fix`, `--core`, `--weight`) |
| `targets/richelieu/render.py`, `final.py` | render letters with a hand-built / final key |
| `targets/richelieu/avenel.py`, `avenel_ctx.py` | fetch and search Avenel vol. III OCR (Internet Archive) |

```bash
cd richelieu && pip install requests && python build_ngrams.py && python solve.py --restarts 8 && python final.py
```

## Conventions

- **Priorities** (George Lasry's advice, 24 Sept 2026). Time goes first to original solutions, attempts to recover a key
  from the ciphertext alone with the strongest model available; second to solutions that match the ciphertext with a key
  or plaintext found elsewhere (a printed edition, a calendar, a key filed in another collection). Targets whose work
  would only be transcribing and applying a key already identified, or transcribing an existing decipherment, come last;
  they still get a DECODE correction when one is due. Open targets are in `catalogue.json`; harder problems outside the
  historical corpus are in `research/mtc3/`.
- Every claimed reading is graded: **H** read from a primary key source, **C** from a known-plaintext letter, **M** uncertain,
  **I** inferred from context or alphabetical position. The website decoders show the grade per group.
- A negative result is only reported alongside a matched control: a synthetic text of the same length, alphabet and cipher
  design that the same solver does recover.
- Before treating a catalogue item as unsolved, check the 19th-century printed editions (Avenel, Camden Society,
  Nuntiaturberichte) and the comment threads of the list posts. Six items so far were already solved in the open.
- **Catalogue vetting bar** (28 Oct 2026). `catalogue.json` holds vetted leads only. What sweeps find goes to
  `research/leads.json` and enters the catalogue only when its `vetting` block records all of these: (1) **seen**: the
  ciphertext was looked at on a scan, facsimile or printed transcription, with where, how much cipher, and that no
  decipherment is on or with it (an archive-only item may say "not online" if it carries `access: "archive"` and
  quotes the archive's description); (2) **prior art**, each searched and its result written: the DECODE record,
  Tomokiyo's cryptiana, Lasry's papers and GL.htm, the printed calendar or edition (a calendar's "cipher
  undeciphered" usually means the editor deciphered it and printed the text), and the literature (HistoCrypt,
  Cryptologia, a scholar search); (3) **key check**: no key held here or in print reads it, so it is not a key lookup;
  (4) **duplicates**: `research/archive_sweep_2026-10/known.py` run on the shelfmark, DECODE ids, correspondents and
  date; (5) **verdict** `open`, with date and who checked. `python docs/_check_catalogue.py --lead <id>` says what a lead
  still lacks, `--promote <id>` moves a passing lead into the catalogue under the same id, `--close` records a lead
  found read, duplicated, readable with a held key or not a cipher, and the bare command must print `result: valid`.
  The bar answers what the 22 Sept 2026 sweep showed (CATALOGUE.md): of 63 entries scored from descriptions, 18 were
  already read in whole or part and 21 were key lookups. Entries below 861 predate the gate; entries 861–1422, all from
  the Oct 2026 sweeps and scored from descriptions, were moved back to leads the same day.
- **Extent: complete or partial.** A target's text is *complete* when (1) at least 95% of its cipher tokens give sense (`fraction_coherent`
  where set, else `fraction_read`, measured from a reading file, not estimated); (2) no gap is left untried; (3) every
  document in it is covered (judged per letter: a group with one letter left open is *partial*); and (4) what stays open
  is scattered code groups or names, or pieces blocked from outside (no key material, too short, illegible, needs the
  archive). Otherwise it is *partial*. (In `profile.json` the extent is `outcome.class`: read / read in part.) The state of the key is recorded separately (`outcome.key`: recovered / partial / none),
  and open nomenclator codes are counted apart (`outcome.codes_open`), because historians call a letter deciphered with
  scattered code gaps. There is no field standard to borrow: DECODE's status labels are assigned by each record's owner
  (Megyesi et al., HistoCrypt 2019), and computational work calls ~95–98% token accuracy "solved" without fixing a
  cut-off (Ravi & Knight 2011: Zodiac-408 at 97.8%). The bar, and the no-untried-gap condition, are this project's own.
- **A reading must read (George Lasry, 22 Oct 2026).** Either extent needs more than a token score: every document
  counted as read builds into coherent sentences in its own language, with only scattered holes, and has an English
  translation. A token-run measure passes a decode made of short real words strung together, so the sentences are
  read as sentences by a reader, who records it on each document (`reading_review`: A coherent with scattered holes,
  B coherent but raw); `docs/_check_reading.py` scores the sentences (language-neutral, a sentence is clean with at most
  one hole in ten words) to point the reader at the weak ones, but the 22 Oct 2026 audit showed it cannot be the verdict.
  Each document also carries the read bar, measured: a document is *read* only when 95% of its words read as sense
  (`read_share` in its profile entry: 1 minus the share of marked holes, guessed `[?word]`s and supplied `[word]`s;
  `research/reading_audit/v10_relabel.py`). One that builds coherent sentences under 95% is *read in part* (24 Oct
  2026). A document that decodes but does not yet build sentences is published as *decoded, not read* and is not counted
  in the extent or the headline figure.
  The audit of every published reading against this rule is in `research/reading_audit/`.
- **Outcome method.** Every target is classed by how its text was obtained, in George Lasry's categories (email of
  21 Sept 2026) plus one for complete existing decipherments: *key recovered from ciphertext-only*; *key recovered
  based on plaintext from external sources* (an edition, a calendar, a copy elsewhere); *key recovered based on adjacent
  plaintext* (a partial interlinear or marginal decipherment, clear passages, a deciphered copy in the same file);
  *read after matching with key from external sources* (an existing key not tied to the document, found here to fit);
  *read with known key* (a key already identified for the document or its correspondence); *read from existing
  decipherment*; *not solved* (attempted; neither key nor text obtained); *not applicable* (not a cipher, or not
  reachable). The first three are cryptanalysis done here. Method and extent are independent: a key can be recovered,
  matched or applied while the text still does not read (George Lasry, 24 Oct 2026: "it is possible to have a key
  mostly recovered but the text not read"). Such a target keeps class *not read* and the method that obtained the key;
  its Results row sits in that method's section with extent "none (key obtained, text not read)", and its badge reads
  e.g. "key recovered from ciphertext-only · text not read". *Not solved* is kept for targets where no key was obtained. The method is `outcome.method` in `profile.json`, with a sentence of evidence (`method_basis`) and what the work
  added (`contribution`: transcription, decipherment with an ambiguous key, key recovered, key extended or corrected,
  sender or date identified, catalogue correction). Definitions: `docs/_methods.py` and
  [the glossary](https://dbourdeau.github.io/cyphersolver/glossary.html). The Results tables, the site badges, the
  write-up filters and the scoreboard follow the profile; `python docs/_check_writeup.py --audit` section G lists any
  disagreement. Whether and when an earlier solution was found is recorded apart (`prior_solution`): a target whose key
  was recovered here before an earlier solution came to light is an independent re-solution and is marked as such.
  A target read by more than one route is classed by its **primary achievement** (Lasry, 25 Sept 2026); when the
  achievements are of genuinely different types, the entry is **split** into parts (`outcome.parts`), each with its own
  method, extent and Results row, and `outcome.method` names the primary one.
- **Words.** "Read" and "solved" are not used alone as outcome words, because they mix method and extent. The pages
  say what happened (deciphered with a known key, key recovered from the clear passages, the contemporary
  decipherment transcribed). A catalogue's "undeciphered" (Tomokiyo) means no contemporary decipherment is known, not
  unsolved. `python docs/_check_terms.py <slug>` lints a page for these and for the style tells.
- Dates in notes are absolute. Sessions are dated so that "since" claims can be checked against the source lists' last-modified dates.

## Publication drafts

[`papers/`](papers/) holds unvalidated drafts, none submitted: [`histocrypt/`](papers/histocrypt/), anonymised
HistoCrypt-format papers on individual results with the official style files and a shared bibliography;
[`cryptologia/`](papers/cryptologia/), a *Cryptologia* version of the Armstrong paper; and [`lasry/`](papers/lasry/),
the material for the paper with George Lasry on how LLMs perform against historical ciphers, built from the targets'
`profile.json` records. [`papers/README.md`](papers/README.md) has the format rules and the pre-submission checklist.
